## Per-concept summaries

### BE DEAD OR DIE (`bedeadordie`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: ta:i; tha:i; ha:i; plei; tɨt; la:m
- AN shapes: matay; mati; macai; matoi; mamatay
- Caveats: Widespread verb morphology (e.g. m-, na-, pa-) in Austronesian may obscure lexical roots. The TK forms are more diverse morphologically; some non-t/ai shapes (plei, la:m, pəŋ, hlai hɨt) do not cluster as clearly and may represent different etyma or meanings.
- Summary: The main recurring mapping is TK t- : AN t- in onset, especially in forms for 'die' or 'dead', with a dominant vowel shape -ai/-ay. Both families show vocalic 'a' and 'ai/ay' rimes, while some Austronesian forms feature a productive m- prefix absent in core TK roots. The match is strongest for forms like TK ta:i ~ AN matay/mati, suggesting possible historical contact or remote relationship, though caution is needed due to verb affixation and semantic variation.

| TK | AN | context | conf | support |
|----|----|---------|-----:|---------|
| `t` | `t` | onset | 5 | tai: ta:i, tha:i (TK); matay, mati, m.a.t.j (AN) |
| `ai` | `ay` | nucleus/rime | 4 | ta:i (TK); matay, matey, mamatay (AN) |
| `a` | `a` | vowel nucleus | 4 | plei, ta:i (TK); matay, matey, m.a.t.i (AN) |
| `th` | `t` | onset (aspirated/unaspirated) | 3 | tha:i (TK); matay, mati (AN) |
| `h` | `` | onset, possibly lost in AN | 2 | ha:i (TK); (no h in core AN forms but present in some Formosan: mahāre, mahāre) |
| `m-` | `m-` | prefix, verb marker | 5 | hlai: m-atsai (AN); widespread m- in AN verbs |

### BITE (`bite`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: kat;khap;kit;kap;kan;hap;cat;ȶit;ŋɐp;ka:n
- AN shapes: kat;gat;kalat;kagat;kumat;gɨt;nut;qəmVt;mit;mac
- Caveats: Multiple morphologically complex forms and reduplication appear, especially in Austronesian. Some AN forms have root expansions or infixes, and occasional palatal/affricate onsets in TK may reflect derivational variants. Deixis/nursery influence unlikely for this verb.
- Summary: The most prominent mappings link TK k- : AN k-/g- and final -t across both phyla, with shared medial a vowels common as well. Several independent clusters with kVt or kVp shapes appear in both families, although AN forms show more derivational complexity. The prevalence of the k/t template in both groups, across multiple branches, supports exploring correspondence in root structure for 'bite.'

### BLOW (OF WIND) (`blowofwind`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: C(V)wV; C(V)uV; CVp; CVw; CVu; CVV; pVw; CW; CVp
- AN shapes: Ciu(C); siu(C); CVp; huyup; ju(p); sup; iup; puyot
- Caveats: Sound symbolism and onomatopoeia likely; some nursery-like or ideophonic forms possible. Variable morphology and derivational affixes (ma-, mi-, etc.) in AN; focus on root shape. TK shows onset variation (h-, ch-, ś-, z-, j-) possibly suggesting folk innovation or borrowing.
- Summary: A strong recurrent mapping is seen between bilabial plosives in both TK and AN (p↔p), often within CVp or CVu shapes, supported by forms like 'pau' (TK) and 'huyup', 'siup', 'ju(p)' (AN). Medial glides in TK often correspond to high vowels in AN (w↔u), and fricative/affricate onsets in TK sometimes match s- onsets in AN. The data points to a robust set of exploratory correspondences in expressive vocabularies for this concept, while also suggesting influence from sound symbolism and possible independent ideophone formation.

### CASSAVA (`cassava`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: man; man_mai; man_niu; man_niw; mu_su; mok_su; man_sam_pa_laŋ
- AN shapes: manio(k); manioka; mangahazo; mioka; tapio(k); tapeoka
- Caveats: Forms reflect recent introductions, borrowing, and regional coinages. The prevalence of 'man-' and 'maniok-' shapes is highly suggestive of widespread diffusion and possible substrate effects; native roots are unclear for both families.
- Summary: Both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian forms for 'cassava' show dominant shapes beginning with 'man-', mapping directly across the families with high consistency. Secondary syllables and codas such as 'mai', 'mi', 'niu', and 'k' also recur in both. However, the parallelism likely reflects contact or convergent innovation after introduction of cassava, not inherited lexicon.

### EAT (`eat`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: kan; kin; cen; tsia/ca:; cin; ȶin/ȶia; cje/tsje
- AN shapes: kan; kuman; makan; kaʔən; maán; bonə; bónʉ
- Caveats: Multiple roots for 'eat' exist on both sides (e.g. bonə/bónʉ vs. kan/makan), and partial resemblance could be accidental. TK c-, ts-, ć-, ȶ- may reflect palatalization or secondary developments. Various prefixes (me-, ma-, um-, etc.) on AN side reflect derivational morphology, not root shape. Data include some analytical/compound forms.
- Summary: Both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian dominant forms for 'eat' often show the skeleton [k]-[a]-[n]. There are also TK ts-/c-/ć-/ȶ- forms that could be secondarily related to k- or reflect other processes. Clear mapping is seen in initial k-, nucleus a, and coda n. Other AN forms (bonə, somiʔæl) are unrelated, showing significant root diversity for this basic verb. Exploratory correspondences thus center on the robust kVn shape.

### EYE (`eye`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: ta;taa;tha;cha;nda;nda;pla;luŋ+nda;lɤk+ta;lək+ta;hoi+ta;lɯk+ta;jʊm+θɐk;ma:k+ha
- AN shapes: mata;mataʔ;matá;ma:táʔ;doriq;roziq;loziq;maca;moto;mɨto
- Caveats: Most TK forms are monosyllabic or compound, with major concentration around ta/taa and secondary forms with preposed elements. AN regularly shows disyllabic mata or variants (mataʔ, matá) across clades. Compound forms may mix roots. Some KRA and Lakkia/Biao forms are divergent or possibly analogical. Presence of nasal/voiced onsets (nda/d) or initial clusters in some branches suggest secondary developments or areal/nursery effects.
- Summary: A strong and recurrent TK 'tV' maps to AN 'tV' (core onset and vowel) within the main EYE lexeme, with both families showing similar vowels and (an) apical onset in the root syllable. AN typically has an initial 'ma-', while TK lacks this outright but occasionally shows composite forms. Sporadic voiced/compound onsets (nda/d, luŋ/lɤk, etc.) may hint at assimilation, compounding, or contact-derived variants. The clearest cross-family mapping is t~t and a~a in root syllables.

### HERE (`here`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: C(V)+nei; C(V)+ni; C(V)i+C(V)i; C(V)+ai
- AN shapes: CVni; eto; C(V)ne; C(V)to; hai
- Caveats: Forms for 'here' often involve demonstrative or deictic elements and may be influenced by deixis/pronoun morphology and nursery/imitative patterns. Some Austronesian forms reflect local innovations or suppletive patterns for deictics.
- Summary: The recurring segmental mapping is a final /ni/ or /nei/ element in both TK and AN forms, suggesting a possible areal or inherited marker for 'proximal' deixis. Medial/inital onsets show some correspondence of velar and coronal stops, but overall structure varies. Results are exploratory: forms are often compounds or analytic, and some similarity may arise from universal deictic patterns or areal influence rather than inheritance.

### MOTHER (`mother`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: me;ma;mɛ;mi;na;ne;nie;nai;nəi
- AN shapes: ina;inaʔ;nay;nanay;ninaa;inene
- Caveats: Many forms are nursery/echoic, especially in MSP/WMP/HLai, raising the risk of cross-family onomatopoeia; both families also show independent innovated 'n-' and 'm-' onsets for 'mother'; functional particles, compounding, and kin term morphology may obscure regular inheritance.
- Summary: Dominant forms in both TK and AN cluster around C1V(C) templates with initial m- or n- and low vowels; AN typically prefers n-, while TK exhibits both m- and n-. The most stable mapping is n~n (onset), with possible m~n (onset) reflecting nursery drift or independent innovation. Segmental nuclei often align as front/low vowels (a~e/ɛ/i). These correspondences are tentative given the high likelihood of nursery origin and reduplication effects.

### NOSE (`nose`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: naŋ; daŋ; laŋ; ʔdaŋ; nɐŋ; mu:k; tiŋ; laŋ; khak; khat
- AN shapes: nuŋ; ruŋ; duŋ; suŋ; oŋ; ung; nose; ihu; sungu; taŋ; ʔiduŋ; ngulóng; iróŋ; izuŋ; sung
- Caveats: Several TK forms are compounds or have prefixes/suffixes; AN shows significant diversity and some possible borrowings. Many AN languages have a ‘duŋ’ or ‘nuŋ’ shape, which may reflect expressive or drifted forms, and the influence of nasal harmony in both families should be considered.
- Summary: The most robust exploratory correspondences are n/n and ŋ/ŋ in nose-related terms, including highly prevalent naŋ ↔ nuŋ/duŋ/ruŋ/ŋoŋ shape-matchings. Vowel differences (a vs. u/o/ɨ) may indicate recurring nucleus shifts but are not consistently predictable. Occasional d/d and lateral/rhotic matches (laŋ vs. iróŋ/ngulóng) occur but need more data for generalization. Both families also show significant diversity and evidence for compounding and affixation in 'nose' terms.

### SHOULDER (`shoulder`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: ba;baŋ;ho+ba;ho+ma;loŋ+ba;toŋ+ba;u+ha;ćo+ha;kan+wie
- AN shapes: aba;abaga;baga;haŋali(h);ʔava(n);afala;papya(l);bambaŋ;varab(o);pag(o);ʔabada
- Caveats: Compound forms and potential secondary processes (e.g., body part derivation, compounding, reduplication) are frequent in both language families. Multiple roots likely in use for this concept; some forms may be innovative, compounded, or borrowings.
- Summary: There are clear recurrent segmental mappings between TK ba- and AN ba-/baga-, as well as coda-internal nasal correspondences (ŋ-ŋ). Onset h(o)- in TK also aligns with h- or ha- in several AN forms, particularly in Formosan examples. Variation in root structure and use of compounding limits confidence for some patterns, but the recurring 'ba', 'ŋ', and similar syllabic shapes point to some promising, albeit preliminary, correspondences.

### THIS (`this`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: ni;nei;nai;nii;nəi;kai
- AN shapes: ni;ini;hini;nii;ina;i.ni;iní
- Caveats: Nursery/deictic roots may draw from demonstratives and pronoun paradigms, with frequent analogical renewal. Forms often reflect compounding or prefixes (e.g., hi- in AN). Some data contain segmental reduplication or unclear boundaries.
- Summary: Demonstratives for 'this' in both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian are dominated by 'ni'-type forms, supporting a mapping of n+i sequences. The presence of 'ni' or 'ini' in both families' forms for 'this' is robust and cross-clade. Other shapes (kai, tike) are present but less recurrent. The evidence strongly suggests a n+i~ni mapping in deictic morphology, but pronoun/nursery influence is likely.

| TK | AN | context | conf | support |

### WATER (`water`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: nam; na:m; nV[m]; lam; ɣam; zam
- AN shapes: danum; nanum; nanom; lanɨm; zanum; a.n.u.m; tubig; wai; waya
- Caveats: The n-a-m shape is dominant and likely inherited, but less common forms (tubig, wai, waya) are derived or innovative. The l/z/d alternation in both families may be contact or internal innovation. Some AN forms (tubig, tubig, b.a.ɲ.u, etc.) are unrelated or highly divergent and not included in main mapping, while a few TK forms show onset shifts that seem less regular.
- Summary: There is a robust n-a-m correspondence at onset, nucleus, and coda between dominant Tai-Kadai and Austronesian forms for 'water', particularly in the kam-sui, kra, and Formosan branches. Occasional l/z/d onsets on both sides suggest possible parallel innovation or contact. While secondary forms diverge, the 'nam ~ nanum' pattern is strong and appears to be a highly stable areal or inherited mapping.

### WE (`we`, gen=4)

- TK shapes: tau; lau; raw; haw; hau; lɤw; rou; ku; toi; diau; cum_ku; śom_ku; ndiau; ćiu; lje; fau; fou
- AN shapes: ita; kita; ato; ito; aita; ʔata; ʔita; kitá; kika; tiko; sika; tika; ami; ramut; mautolu; kāua
- Caveats: Numerous forms are pronominal or nursery with high potential for analogical renewal, compounding, or borrowing. Both language families show significant internal diversity in pronoun forms. CVC > CV, vowel metathesis and compounding are common. Some AN/MP forms presage inclusivity/exclusivity morphs not reflected in TK, and both families have some suppletion/multiple stems for "we."
- Summary: Several segment correspondences recur across TK and AN/MP 'we' forms, especially for t~t and k~k onsets, as well as vowel patterns involving 'a' or diphthongal sequences. Labial or liquid-final and internal variation reflects the murky pronoun etymologies in both families. High analogical pressure and frequent compounding suggest caution, but some onset consonant and vowel nucleus patterns are promising candidates for further TiK-AN comparison.

### DUST (`dust`, gen=3)

- TK shapes: fun;fɔn;phun;moŋ;muk;puŋ;mon;ban;mok
- AN shapes: buk;bo(k);vovok;bunuvun;bok;abuk;appuk
- Caveats: Some forms are compounds or potentially denominal/derived; minor reduplication in AN; likely borrowing influence in Philippine/Western MP area; lack of direct one-to-one semantics possible for some doublets.
- Summary: Recurring correspondences appear between TK f/p~ph and AN b/p in the onset, with supporting matches in the nucleus (u~o) and nasals (n) or stops (ŋ/k) in the coda. Several forms in both families cluster around a CVC shape with labial and velar elements. This supports the existence of parallel segmental patterns for 'dust' in both families, though forms are likely affected by compounding and areal influence.

### FIRE (`fire`, gen=3)

- TK shapes: faj;fei;fai;pai;phaj;pei;pui
- AN shapes: apuy;apoi;apui;puniq;putung;foɾ
- Caveats: Several AN forms are compounds or have prefixes (a-, ha-). Some forms may reflect nursery or onomatopoeic roots. There is extensive allomorphy and apparent borrowing on both sides; not all shapes likely reflect inherited roots.
- Summary: Common Tai-Kadai forms for 'fire' cluster around C1 'f/p' + rhyme 'aj/ai/ei/ui', while major Austronesian forms show 'p' (often as 'apuy', 'apui', 'apoi'), sometimes with an initial vowel or glottal onset. The recurring mapping is TK 'f/p-' : AN 'p-' in onset and a shared high-front or diphthongal rhyme. Partial matches with 'f-' in both groups might indicate later convergence or borrowing but are too rare for confident mapping. Multiple forms support correspondences involving initial stops or fricatives and the diphthong/rhyme, but the direction of analogy or inheritance remains uncertain.

- Caveats: Personal pronouns are highly variable, susceptible to analogical renewal, borrowing, and nursery forms. Many variants and suppletions; Wu divergence on both sides. Some forms may reflect grammatical affixation (e.g., ti-aken, nyaku).
- Summary: There is notable recurrent mapping of TK initial k/ʔ/j to AN k/ʔ/y within forms denoting 'I', with consistent vowel nuclei of -au/-u/-o matching to AN -ku/-ko/-ku. Variation stems partly from analogical and innovative forms typical for pronouns. While no unique pattern dominates absolutely, kV and ʔV~kV shapes and their AN reflexes suggest at least partial segmental parallels worth further exploration.

### LIGHTNING (`lightning`, gen=3)

- TK shapes: lap;lep;jap;fa;pha;me:p;va;laːp
- AN shapes: lat;lap;kilat;ilat;kidlat;lapet
- Caveats: Several forms show reduplication or compounding (kilikilat, koli'yapyap, fa:.⁴_ma.⁶_lɛp.⁸) which may reflect expressive, onomatopoeic, or ideophonic motivation. Some Austronesian forms are complex or phrasal, and there are signs of convergence, expressive alternations, or borrowing. The core lap/lapet~lat~lap cluster is most stable.
- Summary: The clearest recurring mapping is CVC shapes with 'l' onsets, 'a' nuclei, and 'p/t' codas—e.g., TK lap, lep, la:p vs AN lapet, lat, kilat. Several languages on both sides show this pattern, suggesting a possible ancient ideophone or shared expressive root. However, the frequent reduplications and multiple shapes (e.g., fa:, kilat) mean caution is needed, and some resemblances may be due to universal sound symbolism or secondary convergence.

### TEN (`ten`, gen=3)

- TK shapes: sip; sɪp; səp; sup; θep; ɕip
- AN shapes: pulo; fulu; sepulu; sampulu; sapulu; polo
- Caveats: AN forms often contain a prefix or compounding element (sam-, na-, ma-) and show widespread allomorphy (pulo, polo, fulu) possibly due to numeral classifiers or compounding. Several TK Kra forms (hye, put, etc.) and some outliers in AN (ma:nə, masʔan) do not align with the dominant patterns and may reflect unrelated terms or secondary developments.
- Summary: TK 'ten' predominantly shows forms like sip/sup/sɨp across Tai and Kam-Sui, matching AN s-(a)pu(lu)—notably in Malay/Indonesian sepuluh/sapulu etc. There is a visible s- onset mapping, as well as a TK final -p corresponding loosely to AN -lu/-lo. Some variants show fricative mapping (θ-, ɕ-, š- in TK; s-, h- in AN). The similarities are clearest between mainstream Tai-Kadai and Malayo-Polynesian forms, but significant allomorphy and compounding create caveats for full correspondence.

- Summary: Substantial evidence supports a recurring initial p- in both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian forms for 'wing,' especially in CVC roots like pik (TK) and paak/pak/pik (AN). Syllable nuclei in both families often feature a low vowel (a in AN, iː in some TK), and a final k or glottal corresponds reasonably well. Labiovelar and labial fricative onsets in some TK forms may reflect secondary change from a common 'p' source. Several outlying forms (vei, varapema) suggest semantic extension or borrowing.

- Summary: Multiple TK and AN forms for 'and' feature similar simple CVC shapes with nasals and stops, particularly taŋ~t(o/a)ŋ, kap~kag, and wan~kan. These may reflect either recurrent semantic drift among connectives or possible deeper connections, though chance resemblance is plausible given the grammatical function. - Caveats: Forms show considerable variation and possible convergence/borrowing; AN forms with 'rasy', 'ratsy', etc. may reflect a different etymon than those with 'hat', 'saʔ', or 'pjuwa'. Some minor forms in both families diverge notably and are not analyzed here.

Instances of l- and h- in both families also line up with some AN r- or h- forms, but less regularly. There is no fully consistent skeleton, but the patterns suggest several common syllable templates (CVC, V:C) via distinct etymons and possible semantic drift or multiple sources. This inventory is exploratory and does not indicate direct cognacy for all shapes.

- Summary: There is noticeable convergence between Tai-Kadai and Austronesian forms for 'beard' in words with medial or final labials (especially 'mV(V)m' patterns found in both: e.g., mum, humi, vumi). Less frequent matches exist in other clusters (TK: nut~not vs. AN: num-), but these are weaker. Affixed, reduplicated, or compound forms in AN cloud correspondences. These recurring 'mVm' or labial-rich patterns may reflect phonesthetic or regional developments as much as deep etymology.

### BECAUSE (`because`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: jin; yin; jən; in; vai; wi; wei; vi; wai; kɔp_pə; phrɔ; phɔ; pə
- AN shapes: na; nafu; puna; vuna; sina; ania; inia; vununa; mbana; satria; satria; bosatra; satsia
- Caveats: Forms are highly diverse; many TK forms show composed/compound structures and auxiliary particles, AN set includes multiple morphosyntactic strategies and serializations. Some forms on both sides seem analytic or borrowed. No full cognates apparent. Potential effects of discourse or clause structure (e.g. conjunctions, serialized explanations).
- Summary: Several recurring segments can be tentatively aligned, notably TK initial 'j~y~ʒ' to AN 'n', and final/nucleus 'wi~vi~wei' to AN 'na~nia~ña' elements. Labial stops in both families sometimes correspond in initial position, but with low frequency. Overall evidence is fragmentary and points more to formulaic, function-word development than to deep inheritance, but exploratory segmental parallels are present across multiple clades.

### BIRD (`bird`, gen=2)

### CHEST (`chest`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: tak; ʔak/ʔok; pak/pak; na+ʔak; pa+ʔak
- AN shapes: fat/fata; kopa/koba; arang/ariŋ; poho
- Caveats: Many TK forms show compounding (na+/pa+ plus ʔak/ʔok), and AN shows reduplication (fat-fata) and possible affixation; multiple roots likely. Some AN forms (e.g., ariŋ, aragna) do not closely match TK shapes but cluster with -ŋ/-na endings. No clear evidence for shared nursery/echo forms, but variation suggests semantic rather than strict phonological patterns.
- Summary: TK forms for 'chest' frequently feature roots like tak/tak⁷ and ʔak/ʔok, often combined with na- or pa- elements, while AN shapes cluster around fata, kopa, and poho. Recurrent p- and k- onsets and CV(C) structure alignment (e.g., tak~fata, pak~pata) support exploratory segment mappings. However, divergence and compounding on both sides complicate direct correspondences, suggesting only loose recurrent patterns rather than robust phonological rules.

### CHICKEN (`chicken`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: kai; qai; ka:i; kaj
- AN shapes: kakara-; kokora-; kua; akua
- Caveats: Significant reduplication and augments in Austronesian (AN) forms, sometimes absent in Tai-Kadai (TK). Also, AN shows many nursery-like or ideophonic shapes (kokora-, kakara-), and there is shape diversity, sometimes obscuring clear matching. Some forms appear irregular or borrowed; compounding may be involved.
- Summary: Many Tai-Kadai forms for 'chicken' show kai/kaj/ka:i shapes, aligning well with the onset k- found in numerous Austronesian terms (especially Oceanic). There are repeating correspondences in onset (k-), main vowel (a/ai~a/ai), and some evidence that initial glottal/q- oppositions in TK may map to AN k-. However, AN forms are often reduplicated or have added syllables, reflecting nursery terms. The convergence seems strongest in the kV(V) skeleton, but differences in augmentation and reduplication must be considered.

- Summary: A robust pattern links Tai-Kadai 'van/wan' or nasal rimes to Austronesian 'alaw/aldaw/araw' forms, especially in Malayo-Polynesian. Both show a dominant open syllable structure ending in -an/-aw. Other forms (e.g., caŋ, ras, hanian) likely reflect distinct etyma or regional innovations, hinting at a complex lexical zone with some recurrent segmental mapping in dominant variants.

### DIE (`die`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: tVj; taːi; da:u; ɬa:u; thaj
- AN shapes: matV; mate; mɛte; mae; mbu
- Caveats: The major TK forms do not show an m- onset but rather t-/th-/d-. AN forms are often prefixed (ma-, m-); some Oceanic forms have mb- or b-. Multiple etyma may overlap. Some HLai items (e.g. hɔk7, zui6) appear irregular/different.
- Summary: There is convergence in the vowel + final patterns (ai ~ at/et/e), but initial correspondences are less clear: TK shows t/th/d- onsets while AN regularly has m-/ma-, with Oceanic also showing mb-. These patterns may reflect different roots or prefixation in Austronesian. While recurring CVC frames are visible, onset mismatches reduce confidence in a direct segmental mapping.

### DIG (`dig`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: wa:t;weːt;wɛ:t;waːt;ʋeːt;uɛt;ŋweːt;ŋwaːt;waːt;wɪːt;ʋaːt;va:t;khut;xut;pa:k;ba:k;lâu;kon;wan
- AN shapes: kali;kalín;kutkot;kuli;komoih;kmbərih;kmari;kerut;makko:kko:k;magkalot;karkarən;waloko;bijai;βai
- Caveats: Multiple etymologies may be present; AN forms show complex roots and affixation, especially in Formosan. TK forms cluster on wa:t~weːt types but some (pa:k, ba:k, lut) differ; AN shows several root sets (kali, kut, komoih, etc) with possible unrelated forms. Some nursery/onomatopoetic forms or onomatopoeic influence possible.
- Summary: TK forms for 'dig' are dominated by wa:t~weːt~wɛ:t types, whereas AN shows a strong kali~kal- pattern with various suffixal alternants. There is a recurring mapping between TK initial w-/ʋ-/v- and AN k-, especially before -a/-e vowels and -t/coda. Some evidence also points to nasal+onset patterns (TK ŋw- ~ AN km- etc), and minor b~b correspondences. Shape diversity, affixation, and multiple etymologies suggest caution, but several recurring segment mappings warrant further comparative attention.

### DOG (`dog`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: ma; maa; mha; hwa; pha; khuə̃; li; ti_ma

- Caveats: Both TK and AN forms show significant internal diversity; widespread nursery and onomatopoeic roots are possible. Several AN forms (ayam, idu, etc.) are irregular, likely reflecting drift or borrowing. The dominant 'ma' template in TK and 'asu'/'wasu' in AN may relate to baby-talk or regional exonyms.
- Summary: Two main shape clusters are observed: TK forms favor 'ma' or similar labial-initial roots, while AN shows recurring 'asu' and 'wasu'-like forms. A tentative mapping is the TK 'ma' ↔ AN 'asu' cluster, supported across both families despite likely nursery origins. Secondary patterns show some correspondence between bilabial/glide onsets (m/hw ↔ w/v) and rarer alveolar and velar roots, though with less robust support.

- AN shapes: inum;inom;num;nim;nVm;mim;mVm;minom;haruk;le:p;mananu

- Summary: The most consistent exploratory mapping is a final nasal (n/m) element across both TK and AN, frequently with a front vowel and a k-/g-/h- onset. There is also some support for initial plosive or fricative correspondence (k/ɣ/h ~ k/h/g) and variable onset or medial clusters. Coda nasals appear as a recurrent alignment. Despite morphological variation, these patterns suggest a potential set of recurrent segmental correspondences warranting further comparative investigation.

### EARTHQUAKE (`earthquake`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: CVC_CVC_CVC; CVC_CVC; CVC; CVC_CVC
- AN shapes: CVCVCV; CVCV; CVCVCVCV; CVCVCVCVCV
- Caveats: Many forms are compounds or descriptive phrases in both families (e.g., 'earth shake', 'land move'), meaning direct segmental correspondences may reflect meaning-driven innovation rather than inherited roots. Several AN forms are repetitive or reduplicated, and some variation is likely due to lexicalization rather than shared etymology.
- Summary: There is one robust recurring mapping between TK na:m and AN namu/nami/namʷi, both likely meaning 'earth/ground'. Other possible loose parallels include san/sen vs. nunu/nun and phɛn/pɛn vs. vulu/lulu, though these may be due to independent semantic compounding (e.g., 'shake') rather than phonological inheritance. Overall, only the na:m↔namu parallel stands out as potentially meaningful; further data and etymological control would be needed for firmer conclusions.

### EARTH (SOIL) (`earthsoil`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: nam; naːm; din; tum; hum; khəm/kʰum; van/fan/ran; lin/li:n; nai
- AN shapes: darə/dala; dage/daga; tana/tane; lutá/lusa; raga/rara; gabun; saprob
- Caveats: Several forms (especially 'nam', 'na:m', 'tane', 'lutá', etc.) may be independent formations, regional innovations, or borrowings. Multiple roots appear in both TK and AN; this survey ignores possible semantic splits (earth vs. soil) and only explores repeated segmental correspondences.
- Summary: The most frequent TK pattern is nam/na:m, while AN alternates between tana-, daga-/dara-, and others, suggesting a potential n-m/t-n mapping (with some initial consonant alternation). The shape din/daga (d-n/d-g) also recurs and suggests d-in ↔ d-a(g)- links. There is loose support for labial codas and initial v/f/ŋ aligning with r/g in AN, but these are less stable. The diversity and recurrent segments offer exploratory mapping possibilities but also reflect significant morphological variation.

- Summary: The dominant Tai-Kadai forms for 'eight' are pVt-type (pa:t, pɛ:t, etc.), while Austronesian shows two patterns: a widespread walu-type and a Formosan -pat-type. Inter-segmental correspondences are tentative, with possible p~w and nucleus a/a matches, and t/coda mirroring in some Formosan forms. Overall, the match is clearest with Formosan 'pat'-type forms; the mainstream AN walu shape is much less similar to the TK pattern.

- Summary: Several exploratory segmental correspondences appear across TK and AN samples for 'EMPTY', notably coda -ŋ in TK mapping to final -u or -a in AN Oceanic. Onset labials and velars also recur (kh-/k-, h-/gw-/pwa-, pj-/pw-). TK and AN both display forms with initial alveolar stops and liquids. However, overall cognacy is ambiguous; forms are varied on both sides, and multiple roots or derivational processes may be involved, so these segmental mappings should be treated as exploratory patterns, not proof of common origin.

### FATHER (`father`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: ama; mama; tama; apV; amaŋ; ʔamaʔ; CVCV; tV; kVma
- Caveats: All sides show strong nursery/kinship morphology influence (e.g. 'ama', 'mama', 'tama', 'pa' types from both families). Possible reduplication and prefixed forms abound. Morphological complexity and onomatopoeic drift are likely.
- Summary: Several Tai-Kadai 'pa/phɔ' and Austronesian 'ama~pa' types overlap, though m~p alternations likely reflect natural nursery sound shifts or divergent onomatopoeic innovation. Vocalic cores are consistent, with 'a' the dominant nucleus. Preposed glottals ('ʔ') are common on both sides but may be independent areal/nursery developments. Mapping is suggestive but not indicative of a unique shared origin in this high-contact, high-innovation lexical space.

### FAT (ORGANIC SUBSTANCE) (`fatorganicsubstance`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: man; lau/la:u; nai/nam/na:m; khai/khaj/xai/xaj; gu:i/hu:i/ru:i/khui/xui; lei/la:i; ka:u; gwei
- AN shapes: taba/tabak/tabâ/tabaq/tabɨ; sima/siam/ʃimaa; ʔaləf; ima/imaʔa; sasipu; siboq; vondrake
- Caveats: TK forms show several roots per clade, with both nasal and liquid onsets; AN forms split strongly between sima-type and taba-type etyma, and are not semantically/nursery unstable. No reliable vowels match; many vowels appear to align fortuitously due to the small CVC inventory and are not interpreted as correspondences.
- Summary: Potential correspondences include m↔m and l↔l in initial positions, and possible k/kh↔k/q mapping in some roots. The concept 'fat' is represented by different roots in both families, mostly showing m-initial forms on both sides and secondary l- or k- onsets. The mapping confidence is moderate at best, and stems from recurring nasals and liquids; more data or semantic focus would be needed to clarify the stability and depth of these shapes.

- Summary: Segmental comparison indicates a recurring pattern of final nasals (n, ŋ) and the central 'u' vowel across both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian forms for 'feather.' Some overlap in labial, velar, or fricative onsets exists, but these are less robust and may reflect different roots or widespread nursery/ideophonic patterns. The most consistent mapping is the TK 'n' to AN 'ŋ' or 'n' in final position and shared 'u'-type vowel nuclei.

- Caveats: Multiple morphs per gloss on both sides suggest different descriptive strategies (verbs, objects, materials); many AN forms use compounding. Some AN forms for 'fence' include general words for enclosing or wood. Tai-Kadai shows frequent CV, CVV, or CVC shapes; Austronesian tends more to CVCV roots.
- Summary: TK and AN forms for 'fence' show some recurring correspondences in onsets (k~k, p~p, w~w), nasals (m/n), and vowels (a~a), with similar syllable shapes especially in 'ka-/kaŋ-/kana-' items. Liquids (l/r) and h/ʔ show up in both families’ forms, hinting at possible historical or onomatopoeic convergences. Variation in compounding and derivational strategies underscores the exploratory nature of these tentative mappings.

- Summary: The forms for 'fish' show a recurring initial p/b- and core vowel -a- in both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian, especially in forms like pa-, ba-, and similar. Some TK languages display nasal m- onsets, with a loose correspondence to AN b-. There is tentative support for glide or cluster correspondences (pl- : fl-/bl-), but evidence is limited. The overall pattern hints at shallow segmental overlaps but is complicated by likely nursery forms and internal diversity within each family.

- Summary: There is moderate-to-strong support for exploratory mappings between TK and AN involving initial laterals ('l' in onset) and nucleus sequences ui/iu~uli/uli, especially in disyllabic or CVlV shapes. Several AN and TK forms for 'flow' share similar liquid onsets and high back V-glide nuclei. Some formal diversity is driven by affixes and compounding on the Austronesian side, and by onomatopoetic or derived roots on both sides.

The main exploratory mapping is a s/θ-/si type in TK matching p-/pat or f-/fat in AN, with some secondary support for fricative and stop onsets aligning in these numeral forms.

- AN shapes: ma-pia; pia; maya; laba; mali; ena; ŋa(C)ay

The overlap of onset d/l and p/b could reflect only loose iconic or areal tendencies rather than historical relatedness. Formal morpheme identification is complicated by pervasive affixation and compounding in AN.
- Summary: There is a suggestive but non-conclusive overlap between Tai-Kadai 'dai/di/lai' forms and Austronesian 'pia/pia-/bye/fia' roots for 'good', possibly through d/p or l/p correspondences and similar front vowel nuclei. Residual forms with ŋa- and n- may point to playful or ideophonic roots. No robust, regular correspondences emerge, but recurring shape similarities warrant further scrutiny of possible contact, convergence, or deep inheritance, especially for the monosyllabic bases.

The correspondence is strongest for the initial segment and CV structure, though exact vowels and nuclei vary. Frequent use of m-/ma- prefixes in both families adds a possible morphological layer. ### HAND (`hand`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: miə; mia; mɨ(ŋ); mje; fɯŋ; mu(ŋ); mie; foŋ; meɯ
- AN shapes: lima; ima; hima; kamay; kamot; muca; numá
- Caveats: The ‘hand’ semantic field in Austronesian is strongly affected by numeral derivatives ('lima' from 'five'), with basic ‘hand’ noun forms such as 'kamay', 'kamot', 'imá' etc. Compilation includes potential influence from body part classifiers, deictic/nursery roots, and conflation with words for 'arm', 'finger', etc. Tai-Kadai shows alternation between forms with nasal codas and open syllables.
- Summary: Clear recurring correspondences are visible for initial 'm-', and high front/narrow vowels ('i~ɨ' ↔ 'i') in the nucleus, possibly linked to widely-shared 'hand' roots or numeral bases. Some Tai forms with 'f-' correspond, at best loosely, with AN forms having 'h-' or zero onset. Coda nasals vary in both families. Multiple AN forms derive from the 'lima' 'five-hand' numeral base, but a smaller set ('imá', 'kamay', 'muca') align more with Tai-Kadai shapes. Overall, the most solid exploratory mapping is m- : m- and (i~ɨ) : i in the root nucleus.

### HEAD (`head`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: k(V)u; kj(V)u; c(V)u; hV; au; kau
- AN shapes: u(V)lu; (k/g)ulu; ulu; oho; uu; luŋ; kepala
- Caveats: Several forms resemble nursery or sound-symbolic shapes (e.g. uu, u'uʔ, kau). Multiple roots appear present for 'head' in both families. Disyllabic AN forms don't always match the monosyllabic TK patterns segment-for-segment. Loan influence cannot be ruled out for some forms.
- Summary: A recurring k- or kj-/c- onset in Tai-Kadai 'head' forms overlaps with AN k-/g-/kul- shapes, while the diphthong nucleus -au/-ɐu/-ɛu aligns with AN (u)lu/(o)ho vowels, especially in disyllables. Some patterns, like initial h- or glottal, appear marginal. The mapping is strongest for k-...au/ɐu : k/g...ulu/ulo, but multiple roots and analogical formations likely contribute to the diversity on both sides.

### HEAVY (`heavy`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: naŋ; han; ȶʰan; jan; zan; tan; nàk; lak; nak; lan
- AN shapes: mattao; mabigɨt; bug'at; belʔat; malbat; nadammat; maréhmet; mane; mahuna; ʃilʔiL
- Caveats: Sound correspondences are exploratory due to high diversity and possible chance overlap. Some AN forms use affixation ('ma-', 'na-'), and several forms in both families reflect possible borrowings, compounds, or derivations. The semantic field is not nursery/pronoun driven; no obvious roots span all branches consistently.
- Summary: TK and AN forms for 'heavy' display recurring segments like initial n, final k/t, and medial l, particularly in CVC or CVCVC shapes. N↔N (onset), k↔t (coda), and a↔a (vowel) are the best-supported recurrent mappings in a subset of these forms. However, forms are etymologically diverse, especially in AN, and frequent derivational morphology may obscure deeper comparisons. The similarities identified here are preliminary and may reflect typological patterns or accidental similarity, rather than direct inheritance.

### HE OR SHE OR IT (`heorsheorit`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: man; mən; min; mau; mɨŋ; nan; lak; hən; həŋ; kháo; kʰaw
- AN shapes: heja; iia; kōia; ìzy; ɖɛwɛke
- Caveats: High risk of pronominal innovation and reform; some nursery/deictic elements and forms of uncertain morphological segmentation. Pronouns are prone to rapid change and borrowing.
- Summary: No secure, recurring, high-confidence segmental mapping is observable across major branches, but some low-confidence parallels (such as 'm-' vs. zero or 'h-' onset matching, and 'a' nuclei) are weakly supported. Pronouns are highly unstable paradigms, so similarities here may be accidental or due to convergence. The most suggestive mapping is 'h-' in both TK (Kra) and AN (Formosan), but it is irregular elsewhere. Overall, segmental correspondences for the third-person pronoun are very exploratory.

### HOUSE (`house`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: CVC;CVːC;CCVC;CVN
- AN shapes: CVC;CVCV;CVːC;CVN;CCVCV
- Caveats: Multiple roots likely present on both sides (especially AN: balay/baray vs lumaʔ/rumáq̂). Some forms may be borrowings or involve analogical change. Individual forms may have nursery or deixis influence, but less likely for 'house' than for body parts or pronouns.
- Summary: Several Tai-Kadai and Austronesian languages use initial b-/ba-, l-/la-, or variants (with similar vowels) for 'house', and both families commonly have a nasal or approximant coda. Most robust is a b-/ba- onset with an open or nasalized rhyme. Presence of luma~ and ruma~ in AN (Formosan, Malayo-Polynesian) echoes some TK laːn~ and ɹaːn~ forms, suggesting wider possible intersections. Overall, b-/ba- + nasal coda is the most cross-family recurrent mapping.

### HOW (`how`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: hau; hɐu; hu; hɨ; jaaŋ; ja:ŋ; pan; pin; pen; tɐŋ; ku; nu; pe:n
- AN shapes: nanu; paano; sana; kaino; maku; inon; kura; laisawa; lewa; ahoa
- Caveats: Many forms are analytic, repetitive, or idiomatic; some involve question particles or nontransparent formations. Some AN forms show heavy reduplication and affixation, suspecting discourse/nursery forms. Pronoun/wh deictic alternations are widespread.
- Summary: Despite significant morphological diversity and analytic constructions, potential segmental correspondences emerge, such as n-, p-, k-, and h- onsets, as well as nasal codas. Both TK and AN 'how' forms show recurrent use of n-, p-, and k- onsets and open syllables with -a- vowels. These mappings are tentative; pervasive analytic/idiomatic construction and functional layering suggest high contact and flexibility in question formation.

### HUSBAND (`husband`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: pV-;kV-;kwaːn;ʔai;ʔɣe;pho;la:u;sa(u);lak;tu-po
- AN shapes: aCa-;bVn;asa-;man;suami;faʔi;vV-;la(V)k;laki
- Caveats: Multiple irrelevant roots, compounds, and borrowings/mixed analysis; AN forms often use generic terms for 'man' or 'spouse'. Forms like 'suami' are known loans from Arabic in some AN languages. Some TK forms derive from compounds meaning 'male person' or 'my man', and likewise for AN.
- Summary: The forms for 'husband' in both Tai-Kadai and Austronesian are highly diverse, with several roots, compounds, and probable borrowings. Still, p/b-, k-, la-, and sa(u)- beginnings, as well as n- codas, show recurring presence in both families, suggestive of possible loose sound correspondences or cultural borrowing. Many forms reflect generic or relational compounding ('man', 'wife', 'male'), limiting the reliability of any single mapping.

### ICE (`ice`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: nam; nai; nui; moi; sü; tweŋ; beŋ; haŋ
- AN shapes: rano~ranom-; glatsy~glasy~gilasy; vonga~vengana; hau; gon; eis~ʔaisi
- Caveats: Most AN forms for this meaning in Western Malayo-Polynesian (esp. Malagasy) are compounds with 'rano' = 'water' plus an ice/cold nominal root or a borrowed European root ('glace'). Many TK forms appear to reflect 'nam'~'nai' = 'water', so similarities may be due to semantic composition rather than inheritance. Non-Malagasy AN forms (Oceanic) diverge sharply in shape. Possible nursery or borrowed forms present on the AN side (glatsy/glasy/gilasy, from French).
- Summary: Some formal convergence exists between the TK 'nam/nai/nui' series and the AN Malagasy 'rano' (both meaning 'water'), pointing to probable shared semantic structure rather than segmental inheritance. Supporting correspondences are found in nasal/liquid clusters and certain sibilant final elements, but these are bolstered primarily in Malagasy. The remaining AN languages display distinct shapes, making robust segment correspondences rare and likely due to compounding or borrowing in the AN 'ice' terms.

- AN shapes: CVC; CV; CVCV; CVCVC; VC; CV+CV; CVCVCV
- Caveats: Forms used for 'if' are highly variable and context-dependent (conditional markers, conjunctions, borrowed/innovated structures). Some forms may reflect grammaticalization, compounding, or independent innovation. High frequency and grammatical function may obscure original roots.
- Summary: Some recurring correspondences can be screened: final -ŋ and syllables like ka/ko show clear matches, and onset la-:la- also appears in both groups. ### INTESTINES (`intestines`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: sai; θai; ha:i; la:i; ra:i; lam.sai; ja+θi; sɐi; ɬɛi; sha:i
- AN shapes: tinai; tnaʔi; tinaqi; tináy; sii(u); sóeu; sine; sne; nalnáq
- Caveats: The meaning 'intestines' is regionally and morphologically diverse, likely involving compounding and semantic extension. Some TK forms are compounds or contain prefixes; some AN forms show significant affixation or innovative roots. The high proportion of s-, t-, and l/n-initial forms may reflect parallel innovation or drift.
- Summary: Tai-Kadai forms for 'intestines' predominantly show s-/θ-/h- onsets and -ai/-a:i rimes, while Austronesian displays t-/n- onsets and similar -ai/-ay nuclei. Recurrent mappings appear between TK s- and AN t- (onset), between corresponding -ai/-a:i nuclei, and possible l~r (TK)::n (AN) medials, though these are less robust. There is some scattered support for h-/kh- (TK) to s- (AN) in this set. Overall, vowel nucleus matches are strongest; onset matches are exploratory.

### ISLAND (`island`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: ta:u; tau; kɔ; kun; da:u
- AN shapes: lau; motu; fenua; aua; nusa; puna; marau
- Caveats: High lexical diversity on the Austronesian side with both inherited and innovative shapes. Several Austronesian forms are compound/derived or reflect regional innovations. No compelling, exclusive, recurrent full-root cognacy between dominant forms across the families.
- Summary: The most notable recurring mapping is the presence of an -au rhyme in both Tai-Kadai (tau, ta:u) and many Austronesian (lau, marau, malau) forms, suggesting at least parallel phonological development or borrowing for some modern forms. Onsets differ widely, but both t- and k-/p- onsets recur. While structural similarities exist, the diversity and innovativeness of many Austronesian 'island' forms caution against assuming deep cognacy from these surface similarities.

### KNOW (SOMETHING) (`knowsomething`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: hV(u/o); k(h)V(u/o/au/ua/uaŋ); lV(u/o); rV(u/o); ɣV(o); tV; ʔlV; ʔdV
- AN shapes: ma-XX; (C)Vlam; (C)au; (C)an/alam; (C)mu/anini/nili; boC(í)o; su:pəɣán
- Caveats: Many AN forms are prefixed (ma-, ʔa-), complicating direct comparison. Multiple roots may underlie 'know' on both sides; no single mapping dominates overwhelmingly. Some TK forms (e.g., ʔla-/ʔde-) might reflect distinct etyma, not shared with the main k(h)u/lV types.
- Summary: The most conspicuous recurring mapping involves TK 'k(h)u-' ~ AN 'mau/motau', and TK 'lV-' or 'ruV-' ~ AN 'lam/alám'. Rhotic/lateral correspondences are echoed in both stocks, possibly reflecting a shared root for 'know' with lateral or rhotic initials and a low vowel. However, high morphological diversity, affixation, and the wide variety of forms in Austronesian limit confidence. No unambiguous single mapping prevails, but lateral/rhotic + low vowel is a promising lead.

### LAKE (`lake`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: ŋVC; CVC; laːŋ; nɔŋ; waŋ; hou/hʌu/hɔu; tam/ndam
- AN shapes: danaw; banaw; lanaw; lawa; topuku; lebbeng; uciluŋ
- Caveats: Varied morphology on both sides; several forms are likely borrowings or compounds, especially among Tai (e.g., 'thaleesàap', 'tha.le:.sa:p'). Multiple roots seem active for this meaning in both families.
- Summary: Several recurring segmental mappings are suggested, notably TK tam/ndam : AN danaw/dánaw, and a common nasal coda (ŋ ~ naw). 'Lake' shows parallel developments in both families with consistent CVC skeletons and notable participation of l- and w- onsets. Nevertheless, the diversity of forms and probable compounding/borrowing urges caution.

### LEAF (`leaf`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: bɤi; bai; ɓeɯ; baj; bɤ; maɨ; le; hwa; waj
- AN shapes: daʔun; daun; ba-; bu-; vir-; rau; ʔaba-; boloŋ; raqon
- Caveats: Several TK forms show nursery patterns (CVV, repetition). Some AN (especially Formosan) shapes diverge dramatically—deverbal or compounding likely. Pronounced clade-internal drift; not all forms are likely cognate.
- Summary: There is strong exploratory support for a recurring initial b~ɓ in TK mapping to b/β/v in AN, and a diphthong nucleus (TK ai/ɤi/ɨ) mapping to AN au/aw/ao. Coda nasals (ŋ) also align in some cases. Wordform variation and potential nursery/reduplicative origins complicate certainty, but several segmental mapping motifs recur across both families for 'leaf'.

### LEFT (`left`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: sa:i; soːi; θuəi; suːi; sɯːi; pai; pa:i_sa:i
- AN shapes: vili; wiri; ixi; kawili; bibang; wigi; kaliwa
- Caveats: Many forms on both sides show diversity (compounds, affixes, or synonyms). The s/v/w onset mapping might be areal/onomatopoeic or due to chance; multiple AN forms contain affixes. Some TK forms may reflect secondary formations from directional roots.
- Summary: There is recurrent evidence for a TK s- onset mapping to AN v-/w- in high-frequency 'left' forms, with variable but often front vowel nuclei (ai/oi ↔ i/iri). Nasal codas also sometimes correspond (ŋ ↔ ŋ). Multiple forms suggest an underlying semantic or morphological template, but the signal is exploratory and warrants deeper investigation.

### LIP (`lip`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: pa:k; pi; fi; him; rim; min; sop; wi
- AN shapes: pVp; bVb; wiwiŋ; ŋutu; pini; lehe
- Caveats: Many forms are compounds or involve ideophones/reduplication, increasing possible nursery or onomatopoeic influence; many TK forms are complex (multi-morphemic), complicating direct mapping. Some AN forms show clear reduplication (bibir, w'iwiŋ). Overlap might partly reflect sound symbolism.
- Summary: Central labial mappings are clear: both TK and AN use initial p/b/m- and sometimes w-/f- for 'lip', with reduplication common in both families (pa:k, pi, min vs. bibir, w'iwiŋ). There are also minor correspondences for final nasals (ŋ). The evidence supports a recurring areal/labial template but is confounded by extensive reduplication, compounding, and possible nursery effects.

- Caveats: The forms are notably diverse and include several unrelated or derived roots on both sides. Some forms may be onomatopoeic or recent innovations. Many AN forms are compounds or reduplications, slightly lowering mapping confidence.
- Summary: Some recurring segmental correspondences can be tentatively suggested, especially for nasals (n, m, ŋ), laterals (l), and certain vowels/diphthongs (au, o). The roots are heterogeneous and show significant internal diversity, but there is exploratory support for possible n ↔ n, l ↔ l, and ŋ ↔ ŋ mapping in the relevant positions. These patterns invite closer comparative investigation, but are not diagnostic of direct cognacy without further evidence.

- Caveats: Forms are semantically diverse ('search', 'look for', 'find') and show signs of multiple roots per family, with some probable semantic drift. Several putative matches may be due to chance or default phonotactics (e.g., open syllabics, default nasal codas). Scores for weak matches or those with unclear rooting are conservative.
- Summary: Several potential segmental correspondences recur across TK and AN: notably h/h and th/t in onset, and a tendency for velar nasals as coda. However, both families utilize multiple unrelated forms for 'look for', increasing chance resemblance. These proposals mostly reflect surface string similarity, not established historical links.

### LOVE (`love`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: maj; majt; mai; rai; rak; hak; cai; cjai; kjei; ŋai; maŋ; pin
- AN shapes: ma; mana; mai; mahi; aroha; tía; dia; kau; nai; kima; kaima
- Caveats: Forms for 'love' on both sides are highly diverse, with significant nursery, semantic drift, and compounding. Many AN forms contain expressive/reduplicated or verbalizing affixes; some TK forms may reflect verbs ‘like, want’ rather than core ‘love’. Segmental similarity may arise from nursery forms or semantic convergence, especially in expressive/affective senses.
- Summary: Several tentative segmental parallels are observed, particularly the recurring 'ma' root in both TK and AN, and some possible correspondences in nasals and front vowels (ŋai~nai) and onset-k clusterings (rak~lak, cai~kai). However, overall diversity and expressive morphology caution against over-interpretation. These patterns may point to broader areal motifs or convergence more than systematic inheritance, but are worth further scrutiny.

### MAGIC (`magic`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: fa:p; fa:t; fɔp; phi:; mo.; ma:n; ka:; kha:; tha:; we:tmon; kun
- AN shapes: fa-; fama-; hoʻoka-; faka-; mb-; ma-; buroŋ; fai; ⁿdaŋe; nonoŋam
- Caveats: Magic as a meaning is culturally diverse and can be expressed by loanwords, compounds, derived forms, and folk-etymological extensions. Frequent use of fa-, ma-, and ka- sequences in both TK and AN is partly due to widespread prefixes and compounding, which could bias apparent sound similarities. Cross-family forms are scattered and sometimes only segmentally similar.
- Summary: There is a recurrent mapping of initial fa-/phi-/fɔ- in Tai-Kadai to fa-/fai-/faka- in Austronesian, visible in several 'magic' forms. Some forms also show a ka- or ma- onset in both families. However, these are often productive morpheme positions in both groups, so similarity could be coincidental or from later borrowing. Overall, the evidence suggests plausible exploratory segmental mappings but does not confirm direct inheritance.

### MAN (`man`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: la-lahy/li-lahy/le-lahy; lela/leli/lilay; ta(ŋ)ata; wane/mwane; kane; mane; hana; rangahy; remalea
- Caveats: The highly variable roots on both sides suggest multiple etymological sources and semantic drift, with many AN forms (especially Malagasy) showing suffixation and compounding. Some TK forms show derivation from terms for 'human,' 'male,' or possible loan influence. Nursery or expressive reduplication (e.g., lahlahy) and ethnolinguistic innovations possible.
- Summary: TK and AN forms for 'man' show some recurring segmental templates, especially around 'man/man(e),' 'ka(n)/kon/kane,' and labial or lateral onsets. However, high diversity and local innovation reduce confidence in most mappings except perhaps for 'man ↔ mane' and 'kon ↔ kane.' Most TK 'phu/pu' forms correspond poorly to the main AN shapes, and widespread variation on both sides limits support for clear correspondences.

Onset mappings m↔p/b and n↔n are robust, with some support for t↔t and k↔k in less common variants. Most forms appear to be built from a core stock of kin morphemes also involved in other close-kin terms, so segmental similarity may reflect both inheritance and analogy.

- Summary: The most recurring segmental mapping is TK nVn~/nV with AN nVn~/Vna/ena types: initial n and vowel a correspond robustly, with variety in medial/coda n. Both families also have secondary s-initial sets (TK: cin/sik; AN: sini-, hasi-), but these are less consistently aligned. Overall, a nasal-initial, a-vowel core emerges as the strongest exploratory pattern for 'meat'.

### MOSQUITO (`mosquito`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: CaCaŋ; CaCaC; CVCVC (diverse, some -ŋ codas, some -k/-s codas, some reduplication)

Many AN forms reflect reduplication and secondary derivation, and some contain clear nursery or expressive morphology (e.g., CaCaC). - Summary: Segmental similarities are modest, but recurring nasal codas, especially -ŋ, are found in both TK and AN forms for 'mosquito'. Certain TK palatal nasals (ɲ/ȵ/ń) and AN n/m onsets loosely correspond. Overall, there's rare but weak support for -ŋ rimes/ending and some onsets (palatal or nasal) being recurrently mapped, though the forms are highly diverse; many similarities may be coincidental or due to sound symbolism.

### MOUNTAIN (`mountain`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: dong(o/a); vohi-; tɛ/tan(e/y); gunung; toro-/turu-; bo(h)i-; oŋo
- Caveats: Forms are compounded and vary greatly across both families, often involving extensive affixation or combining roots. Some shapes (e.g., 'doŋ', 'po', 'hau') could reflect onomatopoeia or areal transfer, not inheritance.
- Summary: Several recurring segmental matches emerge for 'mountain' between TK and AN: most notably, TK 'doŋ' matches AN 'dong-', and TK p-initial shapes (pja, po) often correspond to AN bo-/vohi-. - Summary: TK and AN both show nasal (m, ŋ) and labial (p, b) onsets for 'mouth,' with possible correspondences between TK p ↔ AN b or ŋ (in different etymons), and shared nasal onsets. Vowel a is common as nucleus; codas alternate between k and ʔ. While multiple lineages complicate correspondence, clear patterns emerge, particularly around nasal and labial initials and open syllable structures.

- Summary: A prominent exploratory mapping is TK coda -p corresponding to AN -t in the recurring 'pit' or 'lit' forms for 'narrow.' Initial -k- also recurs on both sides but with more variability and possible affix interference in AN. Vowels are less stable but show some correspondence between mid/low vowels in TK and high/close vowels in AN roots. Much variation in AN is due to affixes and compounding. The '-p : -t' coda link is the clearest segmental tendency across the data.

### NASAL MUCUS (SNOT) (`nasalmucussnot`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: muk; mu:k; mok; mɨk; khi: muk; nom khat; ko ńe; kwei naŋ
- AN shapes: kokoŋo; kok; mlaŋok; niruwɛkiŋ; hupe; kope; muluta; mare; lèlo; ingus
- Caveats: Gloss is colloquial and potentially onomatopoeic; high chance for onomatopoeic formation and root extension. Some forms are compound or involve reduplication. Final velar stops and nasals in both phyla could be parallel formation. Roots may be independently innovated.
- Summary: A recurring TK 'muk/mok' maps segmentally onto AN 'mok/kok/kokoŋo', most strikingly in monosyllables with a nasal onset and velar coda. Both families exhibit forms with initial 'm-' and final '-k' or '-ŋok', hinting at a possible ancient template or convergent sound symbolism. However, the high variability and nursery/expressive possibilities mean these correspondences are exploratory and not necessarily genetic.

### NEEDLE (FOR SEWING) (`needleforsewing`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: C(a/u)um; dagum; dayum; rahum; djaum
- Caveats: Multiple roots for 'needle' in both TK and AN, with at least one widespread (C-im/m) in TK, and several (da-X-um, Cjaum) in AN. Some forms are likely borrowed or analogically extended; variation in onsets (affricates, sibilants, velars, voiced stops) is substantial. Little evidence for monosyllabic direct cognates; similarities may be chance, areal, or reflect convergence.
- Summary: The most solid recurrent mapping is m-coda in both TK and AN forms for 'needle.' There is suggestive but noisy overlap of affricate/fricative onsets in TK with voiced/affricate onsets in some AN clusters (e.g., tɕ/d, s/dj), though this is far from regular. Vowel correspondences are highly variable. Overall, the series suggest at most loose patterns, with the strongest candidate being the shared -m coda, but evidence for deeper segmental correspondences is weak for this concept.

The TK 'mai' group best matches AN 'bag-/bayu-' types, especially in the m/b onset, but evidence suggests contact or replacement in both families. ### NINE (`nine`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: k(j)i(u); c(j)ou; kaw~kaau; cou; sau; so_laŋ
- AN shapes: siwa~siva; siyam~syam; sia; sio~siow; qisu; sembilan
- Caveats: Several AN forms are loanwords or highly innovated (e.g. Malay 'sembilan'). Some AN forms may come from nursery/child language or contain affixation/prefixes (e.g. a-sia, mamaqisuʔ). Some KRA forms are divergent. Not all forms are clearly homologous or directly comparable.
- Summary: The majority TK forms cluster around /k-/ or palatalized /c-/ onsets with diphthongs involving /iu/ or /au/, while AN forms favor /s-/ onsets with complex nuclei such as /ia/, /io/, or /iwa/. The only robust, cross-clade recurring segment mapping is TK s- ↔ AN s-. There is tenuous support for the mapping of TK velar/palatal onsets to AN s-, and for parallel diphthongal nuclei. However, general segmental matches are complicated by the mixture of innovative and potentially borrowed/analytic forms on both sides.

- Caveats: Wide lexical diversity and complex compounding in both TK and AN forms. Numerous forms possibly reflect compounding and regional innovation; some may be nursery, loan, or descriptive. The AN forms are heavily Oceanic, showing less PAN or Formosan evidence.

Some secondary patterns suggest 'tam'~'ta' and 'wan'~'na' across complex forms, but support is weaker. Multiple shapes and compounding make correspondences tentative and likely reflect analogical creation or borrowing rather than regular proto-lexeme inheritance.

- Caveats: Most forms relate to negation or denial, but functional and morphosyntactic diversity exists (some are particles, some bound forms, some complex). Multiple origins possible; many candidates are likely to be grammaticalized or involve pronoun/negation particles, with heavy nursery/pronominal skew.
- Summary: A few recurring segmental mappings are visible: notably, ma- (often with a glide or vowel) in both TK and AN, and a correspondence between labial stops and nasals (baw/bau–noa/nao). There is also some convergence in palatal/alveolar onsets (tja/cai ↔ sia/seai) and h-initials before open vowels. However, the semantic and functional heterogeneity of negators and their likely nursery/deictic/pronominal influences make genetic interpretation tentative.

- Summary: Recurring TK initial segments like pa-/pwa-, mɛ-/ma-/me-, khon-, and ja-/ya-/ʒa- generally align with Austronesian pa-, various 'feminine' stems like wahine/fine/hine, and occasionally with similar nasal onsets. - Summary: The most striking exploratory match is the TK kVn-/wVn- ~ AN tVw/taw/tau shapes, with recurrent onset and rime correspondences. There is also parallel use of *m- and *ŋ-/ng- forms. ### PIG (`pig`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: mu; mau; mou; mʌu; mhu; m̥u; p(o)u
- AN shapes: babi; mbo; mbɔ; buo; pou; pɔi; poa; abo; boso
- Caveats: Multiple roots seem present in both TK and AN data (e.g., 'mu-' vs. 'pa-' in TK; 'mb/b-', 'p-', and others in AN). Some forms in both families likely reflect onomatopoeia or nursery forms, compounding uncertainty. Also, onset prenasalization is not present in TK but widespread in AN.
- Summary: TK 'pig' forms cluster around 'mu/mau/mou' shapes (esp. Tai and Kam-Sui), and AN forms split between b/bu/bɔ and mb/mbɔ or p/po roots. The recurring mapping is TK 'm-' with AN 'mb-/b-', both preferring back rounded vowels. Correspondence of plain 'p-' is rare in TK but more frequent in Oceanic AN. Overall, forms suggest partial phonological similarities but no strong recurrent root shape across both families.

- Caveats: Many AN forms reflect generic terms for 'sea, open water, shore' (e.g., baybay, dagat, hawa), which are not all directly comparable with discrete TK terms. Potential influence of contact or borrowing; some TK forms are analytic or compound (e.g., 'nam_ma-ha-sa-mut'), complicating direct shape comparison.
- Summary: Some recurring patterns between TK and AN forms for 'sea' include initial h/h (and possibly h/b variation), l/l, and open vowels with glides (ai~ay). However, overall lexical replacement and semantic variation are very high in both families. These mappings are hypothesis-generating rather than evidence of direct etymological linkage.

### SEVEN (`seven`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: CVC;CV:C
- AN shapes: CV(C)VC;CV:V(C)
- Caveats: TK forms are CVC and always end with a stop, most with front vowels, with occasional fricative/affricate onsets not seen in AN. AN forms predominantly have p/f onsets and open rimes (V or Vʔ), sometimes with reduplication or pre-syllabic elements; forms like 'tujuh' or 'wanditi_waitiso' are likely innovated or borrowed. Cognacy is debated; shapes may be analogically reshaped or reflect chance similarities.
- Summary: A rough exploratory mapping links TK front/fricative-affricate onsets (s-, ɕ-, c-, tɕ-) to AN p-/f- onsets, and the TK -VC(-t) rimes to AN -itu/-ito. These patterns recur in multiple clades, though the precise segment match is loose and the resemblance may reflect broad numeric borrowing or chance. Residual h-/th- and various AN affixes represent complications rather than central matches.

| `h/th (minority, C1)` | `Ø` | initial | 2 | TK: hət, thət, thou; AN: pitu, fitu (no h/th in AN forms) |

### SEW (`sew`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: nip; neep; jap; jep; che; faŋ; foːŋ; hoɐŋ
- AN shapes: ta(ʔ)is; tahi(n); caʔit; daʔit; (C)m(a)k(V)s; sais; bet/get
- Caveats: Both families show substantial shape diversity, likely reflecting various etyma, productive derivation, compounds, and convergent innovation. High occurrence of final -p/-t/-s in both families' forms may be coincidence; palatal and nasal onsets in TK may not map identically to the mixed coronal and sibilant onsets in AN. Some AN forms are complex, suggesting affixation. No evidence for nursery, pronoun, or deictic bias.
- Summary: The recurring -ip/-ep (TK) and -it/-is/-is (AN) rimes suggest a plausible, if non-exclusive, coda correspondence in this semantic domain. Onsets are divergent, with palatal or nasal in TK, and coronal/affricate in AN, sometimes aligning (j- ~ s-/ts-, n- ~ t-/d-). These exploratory segmental matches are suggestive but not robust due to the diversity of roots and evident morphological complexity.

### SHIT (DEFECATE) (`shitdefecate`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: khi; hai; ok; u(k); pe; ke; soŋ; paŋ; foŋ
- AN shapes: fa-/fe-; ve-; ha-; he-; ti-/te-; mu-; so-; ke-; ko-
- Caveats: Nursery reduplication and sound symbolism are very prevalent here in both TK and Oceanic AN forms. There is a high degree of shape variability; many forms appear onomatopoeic or subject to expressive formations, so parallels should be treated with caution.
- Summary: No strict or exclusive sound correspondences emerge for 'shit/defecate' between TK and AN, but surface similarities include front aspirates/fricatives (kh/h/x ~ h) and bilabial onsets (p/f/v ~ f/v/b/m), as well as some shared reduplication patterns and similar vowels (o/u). Most similarities reflect recurrent global patterns in taboo and nursery vocabulary, so detected mappings may be due to functional convergence or symbolism rather than inheritance.

### SHORT (`short`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: tVn; tVm; tin; san; tan; din; ndin; tʰjən; tjən; lən; tam; sVn; pɔt; lɔt
- AN shapes: tuŋ; tik; liket; lebu; putul; paʔ; nan; maCVC; CVC-CVC reduplication
- Caveats: Forms are scattered across numerous roots on both sides; some AN forms are reduplicated, and others contain clear prefixes (ma-, ka-, etc.). Nursery and onomatopoeic forms are not implicated but multi-root support reduces clarity. Syncretism with 'small' in some sets is possible.
- Summary: There is partial recurrence between TK and AN in the use of t/n/l/p segments for 'short', mostly in a CVC(C) skeleton. A consistent 't' onset is relatively robust, especially in forms showing -n codas or -l medials in both families. However, the diversity of roots and affixation in Austronesian and variation in Tai-Kadai call for caution: these exploratory correspondences are suggestive but far from conclusive.

### SIT (`sit`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: naŋ; laŋ; sui; tui; tsVŋ; coŋ
- AN shapes: tu(C)–; maN–; to(C)–; –ŋ (coda); –kod/~kong/~kaw (coda)

Not all forms are segmentally comparable (polysyllabic roots, suppletion, compounding in AN). Potential confound of areal and typological convergence in nasal or velar-nasal codas.
- Summary: The most robust cross-family mapping is nasal/velar codas (TK -ŋ :: AN -ng/ŋ), seen in both major languages' widespread shapes for 'sit.' The frequent onset ts-/t-/s- in TK aligns with initial t-/tu- shapes in several AN branches. There is also some parallel between TK ui/oi diphthongs and AN u/o nuclei. While direct root cognacy is unclear, the segmental recurrences suggest possible contact or deep-level patterning for this concept.

### SIX (`six`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: lok; lɔk; hok; hɔk; ɣɔk; ljok; ljɔʔ; jɔk; zok; ʝɔk; ðɔk
- AN shapes: anum; anim; anəm; anum; onom; unum; enem; unom
- Caveats: The major TK cluster is l/h/ɣ/ʝ/z/ð-initial + -ok coda, while KRA forms strongly diverge with m/n and -am/-aŋ shapes, more similar to AN. Some Hlai outliers (tom) are dissimilar; some AN outliers show -i or -o, and not all forms show the full -ok/-om/coda pattern. Possible impact of substrate or numerals borrowing in either group.
- Summary: Tai-Kadai forms meaning 'six' are mostly l-/h-/ɣ-/z-initial with -ok codas (especially outside Kra and Hlai), while Austronesian forms cluster as a- or ʔa-/ʔu-initial with -num/-nəm codas. The strongest cross-family exploratory mapping links the TK -ok coda to AN -om/-um, and there is a weak parallel between TK l/h/ɣ onsets and AN glottal or zero onset. Kra forms (mjaŋ, nam, ńaŋ) are more similar in shape to AN but divergent from the Tai mainstream pattern, suggesting several numeral roots at play.

- AN shapes: tulog; tur-og; tujug; taqi; t-aqed; inep; menek; nan; miror(o); ma-forms

- Summary: A clear nVn~nan correspondence emerges, with exact matches especially in Kam-Sui, Tai, and some AN branches (Shwng). There is a tendency for nasal-initial roots on both sides, with variation in the nucleus and coda. Hlai t/s-onsets match the t-onset 'tulog' root in AN, with similar u/o nuclei. Some correspondences may reflect nursery or reduplicated forms rather than shared inheritance, so further scrutiny is needed, but several segmental patterns recur across language clusters.

- AN shapes: asu; aso; hus-; hilu(k/q); busu-; setroka; usok; emboko; poloʔo

Some AN forms may reflect root-internal vocalism changes or different derivational patterns. Secondary forms relating to 'fire' (fai, faj) also occur on the TK side. Several AN forms are reduplicated or show prefixes (busubus, setroka), and some may be borrowings or reflect regionally innovative roots.
- Summary: Despite considerable diversity, there is moderate support for a TK 'wan/kwan/hɔn/zaj' ~ AN 'as(u)/us(uk)/hus-/qu-...' mapping, especially with the CVn/CVS structure. This may reflect a recurring segmental template for smoke/exhaust, though correspondences are loose and complicated by root diversity and possible analogical or borrowed forms. Patterns involving labial–velar or glottal onsets and -n vowels are mildly recurrent but warrant further scrutiny.

### SMOOTH (`smooth`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: lV:n; lVap; lam/luam; la(u); ləm/lə:m; peŋ; kan; sV; ki:t
- AN shapes: malama; malamatra; mama; maemaea; memehe; lelemo; mambɔ; madojo; mle-; salu; snahlu
- Caveats: Significant reduplication and expressive/nursery patterns on AN side; some TK forms may reflect descriptive or secondary roots. Multiple roots possible, unclear if a single etymon is dominant.
- Summary: Across TK and AN, medial or initial 'l' plus open vowel (a, ə) and occasional nasal codas are common in forms for 'smooth'. There is some recurrence of m and n in both families, especially in reduplicated/multisyllabic AN forms. While correspondences are preliminary due to divergent morphologies and frequent reduplication in Austronesian, these segmental parallels offer initial leads for further historical comparison.

### SNAKE (`snake`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: ŋu; taːŋ; sui; tui; ŋa; ŋɨa
- AN shapes: ula(g/d); u(l)a(y/n); funai; lisu; zisu; urog; ʔulud; ʔuleg
- Caveats: High internal diversity; some forms may be onomatopoeic or nursery terms; possible influence of suffixes or compounding on both sides; some Formosan AN forms show greater divergence.
- Summary: The most promising exploratory mapping links Tai-Kadai initial ŋ- plus rounded vowel (ŋu-) to Austronesian forms with initial u(l)-. This is seen in a cluster of corresponding shapes like TK 'ŋuu' : AN 'ulud', 'ulay', 'uleg', suggesting a recurring segmental template. Additional, but weaker, parallels involve s-~z/l- and final nasals. Shapes are partly fragmented, so regularity or direct inheritance remains unclear at this stage.

### SNIFF (`sniff`, gen=2)

- AN shapes: po(n,o)na; ho(n,ŋ)i; mi(V); ʔu(V)me
- Caveats: Significant lexical diversity and apparent onomatopoeic or ideophonic influence on both sides. AN forms show frequent -ona/-oni/-ona endings, while TK uses m/n codas in CV(C) stems. Possible nursery/imitative origin for some forms.
- Summary: A few segmental correlations are suggested, especially initial h- and m- and coda/interior n/m recurring in both families, with 'ho-', 'ha-', 'ma-' and -m/-n patterns in both TK and AN. However, there is considerable diversity, reduplication, and onomatopoeic drift. These exploratory mappings require further scrutiny and do not yet suggest a single dominant etymology.

### SON (`son`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: la:k; lak; luk; lu:k; lɨk
- AN shapes: nucuk; natuk; netuk; tamaiti; zànaka; keiki; leʔfā
- Caveats: Some AN forms use different roots (tamaiti, keiki, zànaka, etc.), and the semantic field is frequently extended (male, child, person). Not all AN forms are monosyllabic CVC as in TK. Some items may be compounds or not strictly 'son'.
- Summary: A recurring mapping is observed between TK -k (coda) and AN -k (in CVC roots) for 'son', with moderate support in the sample. There is a possible but weak onset l- (TK) ~ n- (AN) mapping suggested by form resemblance (e.g., lak/luk vs. nucuk/natuk/netuk), though this has low confidence. Vowel nuclei also fall in a similar range in CVC forms. However, the AN dataset contains diverse roots and compound forms, limiting the clarity of correspondences.

### SOUL (`soul`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: wan;kwan;hɔn;ŋɔn;ŋau;win;wi;hweɨ;lei;caɨ;hun;van;xan;ja:n;fan;fən
- AN shapes: mauri;fanahi;wairua;varua;ano(a);auŋa;taruŋa;maŋga;ŋgu;mandau;ŋu-na;malauŋu
- Caveats: Many forms show compounding and possible borrowing on both sides. Some TK forms may reflect Buddhist or Indic influence; several AN forms are compounds or possessive with -na. Not all similarities are necessarily historical correspondences; nursery and symbolic/abstract semantic drift likely.
- Summary: There are recurrent correspondences between TK wan/kwan and AN varua/wairua/fanahi, as well as several nasal-initial forms with velar or labial codas (TK: ŋɔn, AN: oŋu-na, auŋa-na). Some matches may reflect shared abstract/poetic templates (labial+vowel+nasal), others are likely the result of internal innovation. Liquid and high-fronted vowel onsets (lei ↔ laumālie, win ↔ wairua) also appear. These patterns are exploratory and do not prove cognacy, but suggest recurring templates for ‘soul’ across the families.

- Caveats: Forms for 'SOUTH' are highly diverse semantically and morphologically on both sides; especially in Austronesian, periphrastic forms and compounding abound. Some AN data is compositional (e.g. 'apa.toŋa'), making segmental mapping exploratory rather than rigorous; also, some TK forms may derive from directionals or compounds.
- Summary: The clearest exploratory mapping is TK 'taj/tai/taŋ' to AN 'tonga/toŋa', both showing initial t and sometimes nasal coda/ng. There is weak but recurring evidence for matching nasal onsets or codas (n, m, ŋ) across both families. However, the variety of forms and compounding in AN, as well as divergent directions for TK, require caution in assuming regularity: mapping is indicative rather than confirmatory at this stage.

Non-systematic alternation of initial consonants, and substratal borrowing influence possible in both AN and TK. Some forms may reflect folk-taxa rather than inherited lexicon.
- Summary: The most frequent mapping is between TK initial kV(w/u)- type shapes and AN la(w)a, especially in the context of CVCVC or reduplicated forms. Nasal codas, especially ‘ŋ’, also recur on both sides. While some forms suggest further mapping potential (e.g., TK c/j/ɕ to AN k/g), these are less well supported and complicated by high levels of reduplication and folk formation. Overall, patterns suggest a partial but non-systematic similarity in certain onset-vowel and coda-nasal clusters.

Possible nursery or imitative forms may occur in both families. Multiple roots may be conflated under this gloss, and not all forms are semantically or phonologically parallel.
- Summary: Several recurring segmental patterns link TK and AN for 'spit,' most notably correspondences involving l-initial and ai/ay-final morphemes, as well as n/d and m onsets. However, the data show significant diversity and likely reflect several unrelated etyma, some compounding, and possible imitative influences. These exploratory mappings highlight only the most frequent and phonetically plausible recurring segments and should be treated as initial hypotheses for deeper investigation.

- Summary: A few recurrent segmental patterns emerge between Tai-Kadai and Austronesian modern forms for 'squeeze', most notably initial m- and k-, as well as a nasal-initial nVn type. Many Austronesian forms show productive affixation (ma-, ka-, etc.), which has no direct parallel in Tai-Kadai. Similar b/p- and n-an structures appear sporadically on both sides, but overall diversity and compounding/affixation in Austronesian complicate correspondence proposals.

### STRAIGHT (`straight`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: saŋ;haŋ;toŋ;troŋ;son;tsɪŋ;so;hjaŋ;zjaŋ;ce;ʒut;nco
- AN shapes: toto;tono;tɔtɔ;mahity;mahitsy;mamari;cemoro;lɔlɔ;nonovulu;roro;ōto
- Caveats: Forms suggest overlap of onomatopoeic, reduplicated, and perhaps ideophonic or descriptive roots (especially in the AN Oceanic forms). Several AN forms have apparent prefixal material (ma-, maha-), which is absent in most TK forms. Tai-Kadai forms are also more heterogeneous (fan, -ŋ codas, retroflex/affricate onset alternants). No clear nursery or pure pronoun forms, but widespread reduplication in AN data suggests sound symbolism.
- Summary: Across both families, a recurring 't/ts/th' onset aligns with 't/to/tɔ-' in many Austronesian languages for 'straight,' often with a final nasal in both (TK -ŋ; AN -no / -ŋ). Sibilant/affricate onsets in TK ('s-, ɕ-, z-, ś-') reflect a possible second mapping to AN s- forms. Reduplication and prefixation in AN complicate direct mapping, but segmental recurrences point to exploratory -ŋ and t- / s- matchings.

- TK shapes: CVt; CVC; CCVC; Cūt
- AN shapes: CVCVCV; CVpCVp; CVCVC; sVpsVp; sup; sip; sep
- Caveats: Vocalic and root reduplication patterns in AN (e.g., sipsip, supsup) may indicate nursery or expressive morphology. Some TK forms (e.g., cup, nük) are atypical and may not represent the main pattern. Repetition/reduplication in both families can obscure segmental correspondences.
- Summary: The clearest mapping is a recurring TK final -t corresponding to AN final -p in roots denoting 'suck', frequently within reduplicated structures. TK s/ʃ/ʒ in onset also aligns with AN s. Both TK and AN forms often show similar vowel nuclei (u/i). There is support for a correspondence between TK onset d and AN s, but this is less strong, and expressive/reduplicative forms may muddy some patterns.

### SUGAR CANE (`sugarcane`, gen=2)

- Caveats: Many forms on both sides seem nursery, compound or non-cognate, and considerable morphological variation exists. The correspondence between final -i (TK) and -u (AN) warrants caution, as do the numerous compounding and multiple lexeme sources.
- Summary: Several TK 'sugarcane' forms show an -oi or -ui rime that tentatively parallels AN forms ending in -ou/-u/-vu/-hu (e.g., TK: ʔoi, o:i vs. AN: ovu, ohu, tou). There is loose echoing of initial glottal/zero onset. While these similarities are not proof of cognacy, they hint at possible segmental pathways, especially in vowel-glide sequences. Broad phonetic resemblance exists in rime shapes, but substantial variation, frequent compounding, and possible nursery usage on both sides require caution.

### SWIM (`swim`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: waːi; wai; wa:j; lui; nam; jou; jɔu; plei; lei
- AN shapes: laŋuy; laŋoy; langoy; lumaŋuy; lmaŋuy; lang; naŋuy; kaŋuy; magaŋoy; huma'lug
- Caveats: Many AN forms include infixes/prefixes (ma-, maga-, nag-), complicating direct stem comparison. Multiple periphrastic and serial verb constructions on the TK side (e.g., 'wa:j nam'), and polysemy or analytic compounds. Some TK clades (e.g., hlai) have divergent shapes. Unclear whether some correspondences are due to chance or shared inheritance.
- Summary: The most recurrent potential mapping is TK w-/j- to AN l- in onset position, with both sides often showing a nucleus+glide -ai/-oi/-ui versus -uy/-oy. Medial/coda nasals (TK m/n vs. AN ŋ) are another trend, though less regular. However, considerable morphological and paradigmatic variation on the AN side and analytic constructions on the TK side caution against overinterpretation. Results suggest at least one promising segmental mapping cluster in the 'wai~l-...-uy' area that merits more controlled historical testing.

- Summary: Across TK and AN, forms for 'today' often share sequences with 'n', 'v/w/ɣ', and a low vowel (a), typically in the NVN or CVCV template. ### TONGUE (`tongue`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: lin; len; lən; lɪn; lɜn; ɬin; łin; di:n; pi; ma; me; mji
- AN shapes: dila; lila; lilaʔ; rila; ridam; idam; hema; hamali; sema
- Caveats: Some TK kra forms appear to be compounds or possess multiple morphemes (e.g., ma_lɛn, măk_lin), possibly obscuring correspondence. AN shows various medial forms (with r, d, l variation) and frequent suffixes (-ʔ, -am, -a). Potential iconic/nursery influence should be considered for frequent l/d alternation. Unclear if all forms are inherited or involve sound symbolism.
- Summary: A recurrent pattern is seen with TK initial l- (and sometimes ɬ/ł) corresponding to AN l- or d- in 'tongue' words, often with a following i vowel. Some evidence links final TK -n to AN -l (through n/l alternation), while alternate TK roots with ma-/me- may match AN ma-/me- in certain forms. The overall correspondence is exploratory, given internal variation and possible compounding, but there's notable clustering around CV(C) structures with lateral/nasal elements.

- Summary: While exact segmental matches are limited by the diversity of both TK and AN tree words, a recurring nucleus mapping of TK -ai : AN -au/-ai/-ae is observed. Onset correspondences are less clear but show some alternation between voiced/voiceless and labial/velar/fricative initials. The data points to convergent semantic compounding and possible root-sharing in some lineages, but no uniform correspondence emerges for this concept.

### TWENTY (`twenty`, gen=2)

- TK shapes: ni_sip; ji_sip; sa:w; le(p); put
- AN shapes: duwa-pulu; dua-pulo; pusal; pusau; sabtin
- Caveats: Most forms are compounds for 'two-ten' and involve numeral compounding/decimal bases. High prevalence of morphologized structures—nursery or pronoun forms not involved here, but synchronically numerical morphs are at play on both sides.
- Summary: A clear and recurring pattern involves ‘two’ in both TK (ni-, ji-) and AN (duwa-, dua-, rua-), paired with a base ‘ten’ morpheme: (s)ip ~(p)ulo. The mapping ni-/ji- ↔ duwa-/dua- and sip/zəp/šip ↔ pulo/puluq is robust and cross-clade, matching the numeral compounding for 'twenty'. The onset s- vs. AN p- in the 'ten' morpheme could indicate different layers of borrowing or innovative replacements.

- Summary: There is partial overlap between Tai-Kadai s-/θ-/ɬ- onsets and Austronesian d-/s- numerals for 'two', alongside recurring vowel sequences (ou/au~ua/oa) and nasality features. The clearest recurring mapping is s/θ/ɬ ↔ d in the initial position, but consistent segmental matches weaken in more divergent branches. Overall, correspondences here are suggestive, but frequency and structural similarity likely reflect broader numeral drift and cross-family replacement.

- Caveats: Mixed roots likely; several TK forms cluster around jouk/juək/jok and variants with k final, while others vary. AN forms show both -ta/-taʔ (~'uta, 'ota) and -ka ('suka') types. - Summary: Several Tai-Kadai forms for 'vomit' (e.g., jouk, uak, ʔuak, ʔɔk) resemble Austronesian ta/ka/ʔota/uta/ʔutɔ(C) shapes, particularly in final k~ʔ correspondence and mid-vowel nuclei. However, none dominate both families; more than one root seems involved, with some recurring mappings (like TK k : AN ʔ in coda) weakly supported. The presence of suka~soka shapes in AN and s/z-initials in a few TK forms is also notable but not widespread. Overall, the data hint at possible exploratory mapping points, especially for coda k~ʔ, but internal diversity and likely borrowing or expressive formations require caution.

Many forms are idiomatic or descriptive, so direct segmental mappings might reflect contact or semantic drift rather than inheritance. Presence of forms with reduplication (e.g., maemaeal, mimlalak, pepeé), and complex compounds (e.g., mwarumwarusiɣa) further complicate the picture.
- Summary: Several segmental patterns recur across both TK and AN for 'weak', notably 'ma(V)-' strings and nasal+vowel nuclei. The mapping between TK 'ma-' and AN 'ma-' is especially well-supported across diverse clades, though likely reflects a common semantic strategy for words for 'weak' (e.g., describing lacking strength). Collocations involving 'nVn' or 'lV' also find echoes between sides, but most other templates are less robust or likely reflect independent innovation or compounding. Further sorting of semantic types (e.g., physiological, defective, lightness) within each family's paradigms is necessary to refine these mappings.

### YOU (`you`, gen=2)

The strongest mapping lies in the similarity of major 'sau/sou' (TK) to 'kamu/kau' (AN) clusters in the respective pronoun paradigms, but the likelihood of parallel evolution and analogy is high.
